2.6.
The Interval Lemma and its
k
-dimensional generalizations

To prove that a given minimal valid function
\pi
is a facet (or an extreme function), we use the additivity domain
E(\pi)
of a subadditive function
\pi\colon\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
. The goal is to show that
\pi
and other minimal valid functions are affine when restricted to projections of
E(\pi)
. This is achieved by decomposing the additivity domain into convex sets, which can then be analyzed independently.

One of the key tools for this decomposition is based on the
Interval Lemma
, introduced by Gomory and Johnson
[
24
,
22
]
. The Interval Lemma allows us to deduce that if a function behaves additively on certain bounded convex sets, it must exhibit affine properties on those sets. In other words, the lemma provides a way to reduce an infinite set of functional equations into a finite-dimensional problem where the function behaves linearly over specific intervals. This dimension-reduction technique simplifies the analysis and plays a crucial role in proving the extremality of minimal valid functions.
The Interval Lemma was later generalized to higher dimensions to apply to convex sets in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. This generalization is essential for understanding additive properties across more complex domains, and has been utilized in works such as
[
11
]
to prove the extremality of minimal valid functions in higher-dimensional settings.

For the purpose of this paper dealing with valid 7-tuples, we will present the high-dimensional settings where a convex set in
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
is given for defining the additivity.

For these statements, we need projections
\mathbb{R}^{4}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
(in particular, to conveniently reuse theorems from the literature). Thus,
define the projections
\mathtt{p}_{i}\colon\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
by
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{1},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{2})\mapsto{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{i}
for
i=1,2
and
\mathtt{p}_{3}
defined by
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{1},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{2})\mapsto{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{1}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{2}
.

Theorem 2.5

(Convex additivity domain lemma, full-dimensional
version
[
10
, Theorem 1.7]
)
.

Let
f,g,h\colon\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
be bounded functions.
Let
F\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
be a full-dimensional convex set
such that
f({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}})+g({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}})=h({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}})
for all
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}})\in F
.
Then there exists a vector
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that
f,g
and
h
are
affine with the same gradient
\textstyle\bf c
over
\intr(\mathtt{p}_{1}(F))
,

\intr(\mathtt{p}_{2}(F))
and
\intr(\mathtt{p}_{3}(F))
, respectively.

Theorem 2.5
can be established in a significantly more general setting, which
takes care of situations in which the set
F
is not full-dimensional
(
Theorem 2.6
). Affine properties are deduced with respect to certain subspaces, which is important for the classification of extreme functions in two or more dimensions.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{k}
.
Given a linear subspace
L\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{k}
, we say
\pi\colon U\to\mathbb{R}
is

affine with respect to
L
over
U
if there exists
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
such
that
\pi({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2})-\pi({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1})={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}\cdot({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}-{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1})
for any
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}\in U
such that
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}-{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1}\in L
.

Definition 2.5
.

For a linear space
L\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{k}
and a set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that
for some
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
we have
\aff(U)\subseteq L+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}
, we will denote by

\intr_{L}(U)
the interior of
U
in the relative topology of
L+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}
.

Note that
\intr_{L}(U)
is well defined because either
\aff(U)=L+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}
, or

\intr_{L}(U)=\emptyset
.
We now state our most general Interval Lemma theorem on a convex domain.

Theorem 2.6

(Convex additivity domain lemma;
[
10
, Theorem 2.11]
)
.

Let
f,g,h\colon\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
be bounded functions. Let
F\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
be a convex set
such that
f({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}})+g({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}})=h({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}})
for all
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}})\in F
.
Let
L
be a linear subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that
(L\times L)+F\subseteq\aff(F)
.
Let
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{0},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}^{0})\in\relint(F)
.
Then there exists a vector
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that
f,g
and
h
are affine with gradient
\textstyle\bf c
over
\intr_{L}(({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{0}+L)\cap\mathtt{p}_{1}(F))
,
\intr_{L}(({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}^{0}+L)\cap\mathtt{p}_{2}(F))
and
\intr_{L}(({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{0}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}^{0}+L)\cap\mathtt{p}_{3}(F))
, respectively.

Theorem
2.5
follows when
L=\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

Note that under the continuous functions assumption, the affine properties can extend to the boundary in higher dimensions.
Without this assumption, this is not possible in general (see
[
10
, Remark 2.12]
).

Corollary 2.6

(Convex additivity domain lemma for continuous functions;
[
10
, Corollary 2.14]
))
.

Let
f,g,h\colon\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
be continuous functions. Let
F\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
be a convex set
such that
f({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}})+g({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}})=h({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}})
for all
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}})\in F
.
Let
L
be a linear subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that
L\times L+F\subseteq\aff(F)
.
Let
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{0},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}^{0})\in\relint(F)
.
Then there exists a vector
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that
f,g
and
h
are affine with gradient
\textstyle\bf c
over
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{0}+L)\cap\mathtt{p}_{1}(F)
,
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}^{0}+L)\cap\mathtt{p}_{2}(F)
and
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{0}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}^{0}+L)\cap\mathtt{p}_{3}(F)
, respectively.

2.7.
Algorithm using restriction to finite group problems

In this section, we discuss connections between infinite group problems and finite group problems. We begin with a discussion of testing extremality for finite group problems. Later we show that in certain settings, a function is extreme for an infinite group problem if and only if its restriction to a finite group is extreme for the finite group problem. Hence, this connection provides an alternative algorithm from those settings for testing extremality and facetness.

2.7.1.
Algorithm for finite group problems

When
S
has finite index in
G
, we call
R_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf f$}}}(G,S)
a
finite group problem
.
Note that there is a correspondence between
R_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf f$}}}(G,S)
and
R_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf f$}}}(G/S,0)
by aggregation of variables, and it is convenient to study
the finite-dimensional problem
R_{\bar{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf f$}}}}(G/S,0)
.
The fundamental theorem of finitely generated abelian groups shows that
G/S\cong(\tfrac{1}{q_{1}}\mathbb{Z}\times\dots\times\tfrac{1}{q_{k}}\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}^{k}
for some

q_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
for
i=1,\dots,k
. Therefore, it suffices to consider

G=\tfrac{1}{q_{1}}\mathbb{Z}\times\dots\times\tfrac{1}{q_{k}}\mathbb{Z}
and
S=\mathbb{Z}^{k}

where
q_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
.
In the case of one row,
G/S=\tfrac{1}{q_{1}}\mathbb{Z}/\mathbb{Z}\cong\mathbb{Z}/q_{1}\mathbb{Z}
is a cyclic
group. Cyclic group problems were originally studied by
Gomory
[
20
]
and have been the subject of many later studies.
See
[
34
]
for an excellent survey on these results.

The set of minimal valid functions
\pi\colon G/S\to\mathbb{R}
is a (finite-dimensional) convex polytope
[
20
]
. Extreme functions are
thus extreme points of this polytope. As we noted in

subsubsection 2.2.4
,
standard polyhedral theory reveals that extreme functions are
equivalent to weak facets and facets. Furthermore, extreme points of
polytopes are characterized by points where the tight inequalities are of
full rank. Therefore, testing extremality of a function for a finite group
problem can be done with simple linear algebra.

Note that there is a bijection
between the minimal valid functions of
R_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf f$}}}(G,S)
and minimal valid functions
for
R_{\bar{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf f$}}}}(G/S,0)
. This is because
minimal valid functions for
R_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf f$}}}(G,S)
are
S
-periodic functions by

Theorem 2.1
.
Hence the extremality test
translates into the following statement about the space of perturbation functions.

Theorem 2.7

(Rephrased from
[
8
,
9
, Theorem 8.1]
)
.

Let
G=\tfrac{1}{q_{1}}\mathbb{Z}\times\dots\times\tfrac{1}{q_{k}}\mathbb{Z}
and let
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf f$}}}\in G
. Let
\pi\colon G\to\mathbb{R}
be a minimal valid function for
R_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf f$}}}(G,\mathbb{Z}^{k})
. Then
\pi
is extreme if and only if
\bar{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}(\pi)}_{\emptyset,G}=\{0\}
.

2.7.2.
Restriction and interpolation in the one-row problem

Gomory and Johnson devised the infinite group problem as a way to study the finite group problem.
They studied interpolations of valid functions of the finite group problems
R_{f}(\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})
in order to connect the problems, but they never completed this program. Due to the ease of testing extremality in the finite group problems, having this connection is useful for algorithms.

Theorem 2.8

(
[
8
,
9
, Theorem 8.6]
)
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 3}
. Let
\pi
be a continuous piecewise linear minimal
valid function for
R_{f}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{Z})
with breakpoints in
\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}
and suppose
f\in\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

\pi
is a facet for
R_{f}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{Z})
,

(2)

\pi
is extreme for
R_{f}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{Z})
,

(3)

\pi|_{\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}}
is extreme for
R_{f}(\tfrac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})
.

The proof can be found in
[
8
,
9
, Theorem 8.6]
and serves as a nice warm-up to the proof of our main result in this paper.

2.7.3.
Restriction and interpolation for
k\geq 2

Some similar restriction results can be proved for the case of
k
rows, but
this area is much more open. Restrictions seem to require the use of nice
polyhedral complexes. The only results known are for the polyhedral complex

\mathcal{P}_{q}
(Example
2.3
) in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Theorem 2.9

(
[
10
, Theorem 4.5
and Theorem 5.16
]
)
.

Let
\pi\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous piecewise linear function over
\mathcal{P}_{q}
and suppose
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf f$}}}\in\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Then the following hold:

(1)

\pi
is minimal for
R_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf f$}}}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{Z}^{2})
if and only if
\pi_{\frac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
is minimal for
R_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf f$}}}(\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{2},\mathbb{Z}^{2})
.

(2)

If
\pi
is extreme for
R_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf f$}}}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{Z}^{2})
, then
\pi|_{\frac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
is extreme for
R_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf f$}}}(\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{2},\mathbb{Z}^{2})
.

For
k\geq 3
rows, it is unclear when similar results are possible.

In the special case of diagonally constrained functions in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, there is a similar result to
Theorem 2.8
.

Theorem 2.10

(
[
10
, Theorem 1.9]
)
.

Let
\pi
be a minimal continuous piecewise linear function over
\mathcal{P}_{q}
that is diagonally constrained and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf f$}}}\in\verts(\mathcal{P}_{q})
. Fix
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 3}
.
Then
\pi
is extreme for
R_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf f$}}}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{Z}^{2})
if and only if the restriction
\pi\big|_{\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
is extreme for
R_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf f$}}}(\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2},\mathbb{Z}^{2})
.

Our main result implies that this theorem holds without the added restriction when we remove the assumption of diagonally constrained.

(12)

3.1.
Special Properties for
\mathcal{P}_{q}

Before continuing, we explain our unique notation. Within our complex
\mathcal{P}_{q}
, there are points, three types of edges, and two types of triangles. When discussing faces of this complex, we will add extra notation to allow the reader to remember which type of face it is. To do so, we optionally provide the reminder in pictorial format in front of the variable representing the face. For example, if
I\in\mathcal{P}_{q,{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}}
is a triangle, we may refer to it as
{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}I
.
In this notation, we may for example express 7-tuples as

Remark 3.0
.

The type (2) corresponds to the translation and reflection between domains.

Proof.

Fix
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}\in\{\pm 1\}^{3}
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
The sets
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}
could be in any of the 5 sets
\mathcal{P}_{q,{{\text{}}}},\mathcal{P}_{q,{{\text{}}}},\mathcal{P}_{q,{{\text{}}}},\mathcal{P}_{q,{{\text{}}}},\mathcal{P}_{q,{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}}
. Therefore there are
5^{3}=125
possible
(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})
.
Of these 125, there are 1 in case 1,
3\times 3=9
in case 2,
3!=6
in case 3,
3
in case 4,
3\times 3=9
in case 5,
3\times 3\times 2=18
in case 6,
4
in case 7.

This yields a total of
1+9+6+3+9+18+4=50
. We will show that the other 75 possibilities are not valid 7-tuples. For the sake of contradiction, suppose
(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})
is a valid 7-tuple. We proceed by cases and handle each case up to permutation of
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}
.

Case 1.

Suppose
I_{1},I_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{q,{{\text{}}}}
,
I_{3}\in\mathcal{P}_{q,*}
for
*={{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}
.

Let
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{i}\in\verts(\mathcal{P}_{q})
such that
I_{i}=\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{i}\}
for
i=1,2
.
By
2.3
,
I_{3}\subseteq[\sigma_{3}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}-(\sigma_{3}\sigma_{2}I_{2}+\sigma_{3}\sigma_{1}I_{1})]=[\sigma_{3}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}-(\sigma_{3}\sigma_{2}\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}\}+\sigma_{3}\sigma_{1}\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1}\})]
which is a singleton. This is a contradiction since
I_{3}
is supposed to be an edge or a triangle.
There are 12 possibilities in this case.

Case 2.

Suppose
I_{1},I_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{q,*_{1}}
for
*_{1}={{\text{}}},{{\text{}}}
or
and
I_{3}\in\mathcal{P}_{q,*_{2}}
for
*_{2}={{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}}
or

, and
*_{1}\neq*_{2}
.

Since
I_{1},I_{2}
are the same type of edges, there exists
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf h$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf h$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf h$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf h$}}}\in\{(1,0),(0,1),(1,1)\}
,
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
I_{i}\subseteq\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}\mid{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf h$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf h$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf h$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf h$}}}\cdot{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}=c_{i}\}
for
i=1,2
.
By
2.3
,

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle\subseteq[\sigma_{3}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}-(\sigma_{3}\sigma_{2}I_{2}+\sigma_{3}\sigma_{1}I_{1})]

\displaystyle=\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{3}\mid{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{3}=\sigma_{3}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}-(\sigma_{3}\sigma_{2}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{2}+\sigma_{3}\sigma_{1}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{1}),\text{ with }{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{1}\in I_{1},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{2}\in I_{2}\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{3}\mid{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf h$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf h$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf h$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf h$}}}\cdot{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{3}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf h$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf h$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf h$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf h$}}}\cdot(\sigma_{3}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}-(\sigma_{3}\sigma_{2}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{2}+\sigma_{3}\sigma_{1}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{1})),\text{ with }{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{1}\in I_{1},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{2}\in I_{2}\}

\displaystyle=\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{3}\mid{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf h$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf h$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf h$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf h$}}}\cdot{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{3}=\sigma_{3}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf h$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf h$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf h$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf h$}}}\cdot{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}-(\sigma_{3}\sigma_{2}c_{2}+\sigma_{3}\sigma_{1}c_{1}),\text{ with }{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{1}\in I_{1},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{2}\in I_{2}\}.

Therefore
I_{3}
is contained in a parallel hyperplane, and hence
I_{3}
must either be an edge of the same type as
I_{1},I_{2}
or a vertex. This is a contradiction.
There are 27 possibilities in this case.

Case 3.

Suppose
I_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{q,{{\text{}}}}
,
I_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{q,*_{1}}
for
*_{1}={{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}}
or

,
I_{3}\in\mathcal{P}_{q,*_{2}}
for
*_{2}={{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}}
or

, and
*_{1}\neq*_{2}
.

Let
I_{1}=\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}\}
. By
2.3
,

(1)

I_{2}\subseteq\sigma_{2}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}-(\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}\}+\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}I_{3})\Rightarrow\sigma_{3}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}-(\sigma_{3}\sigma_{2}I_{2}+\sigma_{3}\sigma_{1}\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}\})\subseteq I_{3}

(2)

I_{3}\subseteq\sigma_{3}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}-(\sigma_{3}\sigma_{2}I_{2}+\sigma_{3}\sigma_{1}\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}\})

Therefore,
I_{3}=\sigma_{3}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}-(\sigma_{3}\sigma_{2}I_{2}+\sigma_{3}\sigma_{1}\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}\})
, that is to say that
I_{2}
and
I_{3}
are translates and possibly reflections of each other. Therefore,
I_{2}
and
I_{3}
must be the same type of faces. This is a contradiction. There are 36 possibilities in this case.

Note that
12+27+36=75
, and thus we have covered all the possibilities.
∎

Let
\Delta_{n}
be the standard simplex in
n
-dimensions.
We can define the faces of
\mathcal{P}_{q}
choosing some
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}\in\frac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}\in\{\pm{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{1},\pm{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{2},\pm({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{1}-{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{2})\}
with
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{1}=(1,0)
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{2}=(0,1)
, as

\displaystyle{{\text{}}}I=\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}\},\ \ {{\text{}}\,{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}I=\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}+\frac{1}{q}\lambda{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}},\lambda\in\Delta_{1}\},\ \

\displaystyle{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}I=\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}\pm\frac{1}{q}\left(\lambda_{1}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}^{1}+\lambda_{2}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}^{2}\right),\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\lambda$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\lambda$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\lambda$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\lambda$}}\in\Delta_{2}\}.

Note that there are multiple equivalent representations, for example,
{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}I=\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}+\frac{1}{q}\lambda{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}},\lambda\in\Delta_{1}\}=\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}=({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}+\frac{1}{q}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}})-\frac{1}{q}\lambda{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}},\lambda\in\Delta_{1}\}
.

We next give explicit characterizations of the
F(\bm{\tau})
.

Lemma 3.0
.

Consider various 7-tuples
\bm{\tau}=(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})
. The 7-tuple
\bm{\tau}
is valid if and only if
F(\bm{\tau})
can be parametrized by the following formulas. Furthermore, if
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3},\sigma
are given, there is a unique
\textstyle\bf t
to make
\bm{\tau}
a valid 7-tuple.

One dimensional Faces:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\hskip-.5pt}rrlr@{\hskip-.25pt}l@{\hskip-.25pt}l@{\hskip-.5pt}rrr@{\hskip-.5pt}r}F(\hskip-0.5pt&{{\text{}}}{{\text{}}}{{\text{}}}I_{1},&{{\text{}}}\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}\},&{{\text{}}}{{\text{}}}{{\text{}}}I_{3},&\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})\hskip-0.25pt&=\hfil\hskip-0.25pt&\{({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{3})+\tfrac{1}{q}\lambda(\hfil\hskip-0.5pt&-\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}},&{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf 0$}}},&{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}})\hskip-0.5pt&:\lambda\in\Delta_{1}\},\end{array}

where
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1}=\sigma_{1}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}-\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}-\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{3}
, and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}\in\{\pm{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{1},\pm{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{2},\pm({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{1}-{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{2})\}
with
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{1}=(1,0)
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{2}=(0,1)
.

\begin{array}[]{r@{\hskip-.5pt}rrlr@{\hskip-.25pt}l@{\hskip-.25pt}l@{\hskip-.5pt}rrr@{\hskip-.5pt}r}F(\hskip-0.5pt&{{\text{}}}I_{1},&{{\text{}}}I_{2},&{{\text{}}}I_{3},&\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})\hskip-0.25pt&=\hfil\hskip-0.25pt&\{({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{3})+\tfrac{1}{q}\lambda(\hfil\hskip-0.5pt&-\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{1},&\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{2},&{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{1}-{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{2})\hskip-0.5pt&:\lambda\in\Delta_{1}\},\end{array}

where
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1}=\sigma_{1}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}-\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}-\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{3}
,
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{1}=(1,0)
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{2}=(0,1)
.

Two dimensional Faces:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\hskip-.5pt}rrlr@{\hskip-.25pt}l@{\hskip-.25pt}l@{\hskip-.5pt}rrr@{\hskip-.5pt}r}F(\hskip-0.5pt&{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}I_{1},&{{\text{}}}\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf where
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1}=\sigma_{1}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}-\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}-\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{3}
, and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{2}\in\{\pm{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{1},\pm{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{2},\pm({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{1}-{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{2})\}
,
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1}\neq{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{2}
.

\begin{array}[]{r@{\hskip-.5pt}rrlr@{\hskip-.25pt}l@{\hskip-.25pt}l@{\hskip-.5pt}rrr@{\hskip-.5pt}r}F(\hskip-0.5pt&{{\text{}}}I_{1},&{{\text{}}}I_{2},&{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}I_{3},&\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf where
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{3}=-{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}
,
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1}=\sigma{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{1},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{2}=\sigma{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{2},\sigma\in\{\pm 1\}
.

We save the proof for the appendix.

Lemma 3.0
.

Suppose that
K_{1},K_{2}
are edges of
K
and
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\mu$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\mu$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\mu$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\mu$}}=(1,1,-1)
.
The 7-tuple
\bm{\tau}=(K_{1},K_{2},K,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\mu$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\mu$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\mu$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\mu$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})
is valid if and only if
F(\bm{\tau})
can be parametrized by the following formulas:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\hskip-.5pt}rrlr@{\hskip-.25pt}l@{\hskip-.25pt}l@{\hskip-.5pt}rrr@{\hskip-.5pt}r}F(\hskip-0.5pt&{{\text{}}}K_{1},&{{\text{}}}K_{2},&{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}K,&\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\mu$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\mu$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\mu$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\mu$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})+\frac{1}{q}(\hfil\hskip-0.5pt&\lambda_{1}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf where
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{i}
is a direction of
K_{i}
such that
\textstyle\bf t
is the vertex of
K
in the intersection of the two directions and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}+\frac{1}{q}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}+\frac{1}{q}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{2}
are the other two vertices.

Lemma 3.0
.

Suppose
(I,J,K,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})
is a valid 7-tuple with different types of edges
I,J\in\mathcal{P}_{q,{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}}
and a triangle
K\in\mathcal{P}_{q,{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}}
. Then there exists
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}\in\verts(J)
such that
(I,\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}\},K_{1},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})
is a valid 7-tuple, where
K_{1}
is an edge of
K
with the same type as
I
.

Proof.

We will prove this for the case where
I\in\mathcal{P}_{q,{{\text{}}}}
,
J\in\mathcal{P}_{q,{{\text{}}}}
. The other cases are similar so we will omit their proofs.
By
3.0
, we can parametrize

F({{\text{}}}I,{{\text{}}}J,{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}K,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})=\{(\sigma_{1}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1},\sigma_{2}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2},-\sigma_{3}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{3})+\frac{1}{q}(\lambda_{1}\sigma_{1}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}^{1},\lambda_{2}\sigma_{2}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}^{2},-\sigma_{3}(\lambda_{1}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}^{1}+\lambda_{2}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}^{2})):\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\lambda$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\lambda$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\lambda$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\lambda$}}\in\Delta_{2}\},

where
\sigma_{2}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}
,
\sigma_{2}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}+\frac{1}{q}\lambda_{2}\sigma_{2}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{2}
are two vertices of
J
, and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}-{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{3}
.

Then
K_{1}=\{-\sigma_{3}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{3}-\frac{1}{q}\sigma_{3}\lambda_{1}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1}:\lambda_{1}\in[0,1]\}
,
\sigma_{1}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1}=\sigma_{1}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}-\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}(\sigma_{2}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2})-\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}(-\sigma_{3}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{3})
,
and for
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}=-\sigma_{3}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1}
,

F(I,\{\sigma_{2}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}\},K_{1},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})=\{(\sigma_{1}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1},\sigma_{2}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2},-\sigma_{3}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{3})+\tfrac{1}{q}\lambda(-\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf 0$}}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}):\lambda\in\Delta_{1}\}.

Therefore,
(I,\{\sigma_{2}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}\},K_{1},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})
is a valid 7-tuple.
∎

3.2.
Triangulation properties

The specific choice of the triangulation
\mathcal{P}_{q}
lends itself to strong unimodularity properties.
This will allow us to demonstrate that the important additivities can be written using the original faces of the complex
\mathcal{P}_{q}
.

We begin with a structural lemma about
\mathcal{P}_{q}
regarding Minkowski sums that seems to be a key ingredient in preserving the structure of the complex under projections of additive faces.

Lemma 3.0

(Lemma 4.3,
[
10
]
)
.

Let
I,J\in\mathcal{P}_{q}
. Then
-I
and
I+J
are unions of faces in
\mathcal{P}_{q}
.

Consider a pair of matrices
(A,B)
with
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times p}
and
B\in\mathbb{R}^{m^{\prime}\times p}
. For an integer
n
, the matrix
{A\choose B}^{(n)}
is the
n
-fold matrix is the
(n\cdot m+m^{\prime})\times(n\cdot p)
block structured matrix

{A\choose B}^{(n)}:=\begin{pmatrix}A&\\
&A\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&A\\
B&B&\cdots&B\end{pmatrix}

Lemma 3.0
.

Let
A
be defined in (
11
) and
consider the polyhedron

P=\{({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{1},\dots,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{n}):Ax^{i}\leq{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{i}\text{ for }i=1,\dots,n,\text{ and }\sum_{i=1}^{p}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{i}-\sum_{i={p+1}}^{n}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{i}=\bf{0}\}

If
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf b$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf b$}}}^{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{6}
for all
i=1\dots,n
, then
P
is an integral polyhedron (as a consequence of its constraint matrix being totally unimodular). Furthermore, the projections
p_{i}(P)
onto the variables
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{i}
are all integral polyhedra as well.

Proof.

Because totally unimodularity is preserved under the operations multiplying a row or column by
-1
, adding or removing a repeated row or column, and adding or removing a row or column that is standard unit vector, we only need to show that
{\bar{A}\choose I}^{(n)}
is totally unimodular for
\bar{A}=\begin{bmatrix}1&1\end{bmatrix}
. Note that this is a
(0,1)
-matrix with at most two nonzeros per column.
By
[
25
]
(also see,
[
15
, Corollary 4.8]
), we know that it is totally unimodular because it has an equitable bicoloring, i.e., the rows corresponding to
\bar{A}
is one color and the rows corresponding to
I
is another color.
∎

The properties in
3.0
provide an easy method to compute
E(\pi,\mathcal{P}_{q})

by using simple arithmetic and set
membership operations on vertices of
\mathcal{P}_{q}
.

Lemma 3.0

(Variation of
[
10
, Lemma 4.4]
.
See also
[
8
,
9
, Lemma 7.3]
)
.

Let
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}
be faces in the complex
\mathcal{P}_{q}
,
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}\in\{-1,0,1\}^{3}
, and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}\in\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Let
F=F(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})
.

(i)

The projections
p_{1}(F)
,
p_{2}(F)
, and
p_{3}(F)
are faces in the complex
\mathcal{P}_{q}
. Call these faces
I^{\prime}_{1},I^{\prime}_{2},I^{\prime}_{3}
, respectively. Then

F=F(I^{\prime}_{1},I^{\prime}_{2},I^{\prime}_{3},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})
.

(ii)

The vertices of
F
are in
\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{2}\times\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{2}\times\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

Proof.

By definition of
F
, we know that
F=F(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})
for some
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}\in\mathcal{P}_{q}
. From
2.3
, we have the following explicit formulas for the projections:

\displaystyle p_{1}(F)

\displaystyle=I_{1}\cap\left[\sigma_{1}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}-\left(\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}I_{2}+\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}I_{3}\right)\right],

\displaystyle p_{2}(F)

\displaystyle=I_{2}\cap\left[\sigma_{2}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}-\left(\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}I_{1}+\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}I_{3}\right)\right],

\displaystyle p_{3}(F)

\displaystyle=I_{3}\cap\left[\sigma_{3}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}-\left(\sigma_{3}\sigma_{2}I_{2}+\sigma_{3}\sigma_{1}I_{1}\right)\right].

To show that
p_{1}(F)
is a face of
\mathcal{P}_{q}
, note that
I_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{q}
by assumption. Through multiple applications of
3.0
, it follows that
\left[\sigma_{1}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}-\left(\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}I_{2}+\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}I_{3}\right)\right]
is a union of faces of
\mathcal{P}_{q}
. Since
\mathcal{P}_{q}
is a polyhedral complex (and hence closed under intersections), the intersection of
I_{1}
with this union of faces is a convex union of faces that is a subset of
I_{1}
, and thus must be a face of
I_{1}
, and therefore a face of
\mathcal{P}_{q}
. Thus,
I^{\prime}_{1}:=p_{1}(F)
is a face of
\mathcal{P}_{q}
. This follows similarly for
I^{\prime}_{2}=p_{2}(F)
and
I^{\prime}_{3}=p_{3}(F)
.

Therefore,
F\subseteq I^{\prime}_{1}\times I^{\prime}_{2}\times I^{\prime}_{3}\subseteq I_{1}\times I_{2}\times I_{3}
. It follows that
F=F(I^{\prime}_{1},I^{\prime}_{2},I^{\prime}_{3},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})
.

The intersection with the translated scaled sets
\sigma_{1}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}-(\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}I_{2}+\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}I_{3})
preserves the structure of
I_{1}
because the translation and scaling operations do not introduce elements outside
\mathcal{P}_{q}
. Hence,
p_{1}(F)
is a face of
\mathcal{P}_{q}
. The same argument applies to
p_{2}(F)
and
p_{3}(F)
.

Part (ii) follows from
3.0
.
∎

4.
Interval Lemma - Applications and Hidden Functional Equation

Now that we understand the faces that describe possibile additivies and generalized descriptions of these additivities, we can apply
2.6
to understand its implications.

Applying to the valid 7-tuple, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 4.0
.

If
\bar{\pi}
is continuous and additive over the valid tuple
({{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}I,{{\text{}}}J,{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}K,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf 0$}}})
, then
\bar{\pi}
is affine over
I,J,K
in the
-direction.

Proof.

Apply
2.6
on
F=\{({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}})\mid{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}\in\sigma_{1}I,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}\in\sigma_{2}J,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}\in-\sigma_{3}K\}
and
L=\{(x,0)\mid x\in\mathbb{R}\}
corresponding to the edge type of
J
. Because the tuple is valid, we have
\mathtt{p}_{1}(F)=\sigma_{1}I,\mathtt{p}_{2}(F)=\sigma_{2}J,\mathtt{p}_{3}(F)=-\sigma_{3}K
.
∎

4.1.
Hidden Cauchy functional equation through multiple relations

In prior work, it sufficed to consider additives in tuples individually when applying functional equation results. Here, however, we need to see how multiple additivities can interact.
We combine relations to find a hidden Cauchy Functional Equation on Additive Domains. In particular, when relations have overlapping projections. See for instance Figure
4
where the projection
\mathtt{p}_{3}
overlaps for two additivities.
