Lemma 4.0

(Hidden Interval Lemma)
.

Let
f,g,h\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
be continuous functions. Let
F,\tilde{F}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
convex
such that
f({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}})+g({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}})=h({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}})
for all
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}})\in F\cup\tilde{F}
.

Suppose that
\mathtt{p}_{3}(F)=\mathtt{p}_{3}(\tilde{F})
and is full dimensional in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Furthermore, suppose that

\mathtt{p}_{i}(F)=\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{i}+\lambda_{i}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{i}:\lambda_{i}\in[0,1]\},\ \ \text{ and }\mathtt{p}_{i}(\tilde{F})=\{\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{i}+\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{i}:\tilde{\lambda}_{i}\in[0,1]\}\ \ \text{ for }i=1,2

such that
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1}=\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1}
.
That is, the projections
\mathtt{p}_{1}
for both convex sets
F,\tilde{F}
are simply translates of each other.

Furthermore, assume that the pairs
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{2})
,
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1},\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{2})
, and
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{2},\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{2})

are all linearly independent.

Then there exists a vector
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
f,g
and
h
are affine with gradient
\textstyle\bf c
with respect to the linear space defined by
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1}
over
\mathtt{p}_{i}(F)
and
\mathtt{p}_{i}(\tilde{F})
for
i=1,2,3
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we will assume that
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1}=\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{1}
.
Let

D=\begin{bmatrix}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1}&{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{2}\end{bmatrix},\text{ and }\tilde{D}=\begin{bmatrix}\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1}&\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{2}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1}&\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{2}\end{bmatrix}.

Since
\mathtt{p}_{i}(F),\mathtt{p}_{i}(\tilde{F})
are line segments for
i=1,2
, any point
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}\in\mathtt{p}_{3}(F)=\mathtt{p}_{3}(\tilde{F})
can be written uniquely as

{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}+D\bm{\lambda}\ \ \ \text{ or equivalently }\ \ \ {\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}=\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1}+\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}+\tilde{D}\tilde{\bm{\lambda}},

where
\bm{\lambda},\tilde{\bm{\lambda}}\in[0,1]^{2}
.
Setting these relations equal, we can derive an expression for
\tilde{\bm{\lambda}}
in terms of
\bm{\lambda}
as

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{\lambda}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{D}^{-1}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}-\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1}-\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2})+\tilde{D}^{-1}D\bm{\lambda}=\bar{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}+\begin{bmatrix}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf e$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf e$}}}^{1}&\bar{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}}\end{bmatrix}\bm{\lambda}

for appropriate choices of
\bar{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}}
and
\bar{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}}
.

So

\tilde{\lambda}_{1}=\bar{u}_{1}+\lambda_{1}+\bar{d}_{1}\lambda_{2}
and
\tilde{\lambda}_{2}=\bar{u}_{2}+\bar{d}_{2}\lambda_{2}.

Next, by substitution,

\displaystyle f({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1}+\lambda_{1}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1})+g({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}+\lambda_{2}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{2})

\displaystyle=h({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}+D\bm{\lambda})

\displaystyle=h(\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}}^{1}+\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}+\tilde{D}\tilde{\bm{\lambda}})

\displaystyle=f(\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1}+\tilde{\lambda}_{1}\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1})+g(\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}+\tilde{\lambda}_{2}\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{2})

\displaystyle=f(\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1}+\left(\bar{u}_{1}+\lambda_{1}+\bar{d}_{1}\lambda_{2}\right)\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1})+g(\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}+\left(\bar{u}_{2}+\bar{d}_{2}\lambda_{2}\right)\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{2})

Rewriting, we have

\underbrace{f({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1}+\lambda_{1}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1})}_{\hat{f}(\lambda_{1})}+\underbrace{g({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}+\lambda_{2}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{2})-g(\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}+\left(\bar{u}_{2}+\bar{d}_{2}\lambda_{2}\right)\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{2})}_{\hat{g}(\lambda_{2})}=\underbrace{f(\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1}+\left(\bar{u}_{1}+\lambda_{1}+\bar{d}_{1}\lambda_{2}\right)\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1})}_{\hat{h}(\lambda_{1}+\bar{d}_{1}\lambda_{2})}.

Moreover, this holds for all
\{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in[0,1]^{2}:\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\leq 1\}
.
By applying
Theorem 2.5
to
\hat{f},\hat{g},\hat{h}
, we know that

\displaystyle f({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1}+\lambda_{1}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1})

\displaystyle=c\lambda_{1}

\displaystyle g({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}+\lambda_{2}(\bar{d}_{1}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1}+\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}}^{2}))-g(\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}+\tilde{\lambda}_{2}\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{2})

\displaystyle=g({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}+\lambda_{2}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{2})-g(\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{2}+\left(\bar{u}_{2}+\bar{d}_{2}\lambda_{2}\right)\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{2})=c\bar{d}_{1}\lambda_{2}

\displaystyle f(\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1}+\tilde{\lambda}_{1}\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1})

\displaystyle=f(\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{1}+\left(\bar{u}_{1}+\lambda_{1}+\bar{d}_{1}\lambda_{2}\right)\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1})=c(\lambda_{1}+\bar{d}_{1}\lambda_{2}).

Thus,
f
is affine with gradient
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}:=c\,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1}
over
\mathtt{p}_{1}(F)
and
\mathtt{p}_{1}(\bar{F})
, and
g
is affine with gradient
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}:=c\,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1}
with respect to the linear space
\mathrm{span}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1})
over
\mathtt{p}_{2}(F)
and
\mathtt{p}_{2}(\bar{F})
. By additivity, we know that
h
is affine with gradient
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf c$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf c$}}}:=c\,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1}
with respect to the linear space
\mathrm{span}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{1})
over
\mathtt{p}_{3}(F)=\mathtt{p}_{3}(\bar{F})
.
∎

(Interval Lemma on Edges)
.

Pick distinct

.
Suppose
\bar{\pi}
is additive over
\bm{\tau}^{1}=(I_{1},J_{1},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}K,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}^{1},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}^{1})
and
\bm{\tau}^{2}=(I_{2},J_{2},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}K,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}^{2},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}^{2})
with
K\in\mathcal{P}_{q,{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}}
,
I_{1},I_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{q,{\text{}}}
,
J_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{q,{\text{}}}
and
J_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{q,{\text{}}}
. Then
\pi
is affine in the
direction in the faces
I_{1},I_{2}
and
K
.

5.
Underlying theory of perturbations with finite reflection groups

We prove a fundamental lemma underlying some of the concepts for proofs in our papers on equivariant perturbations for the infinite group problem. We do this without defining anything about the infinite group problem and instead, just show how certain function spaces cut by linear equations decompose to an infinite set of finite dimensional linear systems.

We say that the domain
D\subseteq[0,1]
is
symmetric
if
D=1-D:=\{1-x:x\in D\}
.
Let
K
be a finite set and let
(a_{k},b_{k})\in\mathbb{R}
for all
k\in K
. Let
I_{k},J_{k}\subseteq[\ell]:=\{1,2,\dots,\ell\}
for
k\in K
, where
\ell
is a positive integer.
Consider the function space defined by linear equations parametrized by the symmetric domain
D
:

(13)

\mathcal{F}(D):=\left\{f\colon D\times[\ell]\to\mathbb{R}:\sum_{i\in I_{k}}a_{i}f(x,i)+\sum_{j\in J_{k}}b_{j}f(1-x,j)=0\quad\forall\;x\in D,\;k\in K\right\}.

Define

(14)

\Delta_{k}f(x):=\sum_{i\in I_{k}}a_{i}{f}(x,i)+\sum_{j\in J_{k}}b_{j}{f}(1-x,j).

Definition 5.0
.

The
orbit
of a point
x\in[0,1]
, denoted by
\mathcal{O}(x)
, is defined as:

\mathcal{O}(x)=\{(x,i),(1-x,i):i\in[\ell]\}.

The orbit includes both the point
x
and its reflection
1-x
, and all the indices in
[\ell]
. Note that the orbit of
x=1/2
is
\mathcal{O}(1/2)=\{(1/2,i):i\in[\ell]\}
.

Lemma 5.0
.

The orbits
\mathcal{O}(x)
for points
x\in[0,1]
have the following properties:

(1)

For any
x\in[0,1]
, we have
\mathcal{O}(x)=\mathcal{O}(1-x)
, meaning the system behaves symmetrically with respect to
x
and
1-x
.

(2)

The orbits of distinct points
x
and
x^{\prime}
in the interval
[0,1/2]
are disjoint:

\mathcal{O}(x)\cap\mathcal{O}(x^{\prime})=\emptyset\quad\text{for}\;x\neq x^{\prime},\;x,x^{\prime}\in[0,1/2].

(3)

The set of all points
(x,i)
for
x\in[0,1]
and
i=1,\dots,\ell
can be expressed as the union of orbits for
x\in[0,1/2]
:

\{(x,i):x\in[0,1],i\in[\ell]\}=\bigsqcup_{x\in[0,1/2]}\mathcal{O}(x).

The proof is somewhat trivial, so we provide it only in the appendix.

We next prove our main result of the section. For a domain
D\subseteq[0,1]
function
f
with
f\colon D\times[\ell]\to\mathbb{R}
, we say that
f
is nontrivial if
f\not\equiv 0
. We say that
f
is piecewise linear if each function
f(\cdot,i)
is piecewise linear for
i\in[\ell]
.

Lemma 5.0

(Nontrivial Infinite System
\iff
Nontrivial Finite System)
.

For any integer
m>2
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

There exists a nontrivial solution for
\mathcal{F}([0,1])
.

(2)

There exists a nontrivial continuous piecewise linear solution for
\mathcal{F}([0,1])
with
f(0,i)=f(1,i)=0
for all
i=1,\dots,\ell
.

(3)

There exists a nontrivial solution for
\mathcal{F}(\{\tfrac{1}{m},\tfrac{m-1}{m}\})
.

Proof.

We show
(2)\Rightarrow(1)\Rightarrow(3)\Rightarrow(2)
.

Step 1.
Clearly
(2)\Rightarrow(1)
.

Step 2.
We show that
(3)\Rightarrow(2)
.

Suppose there exists a nontrivial solution
f
for
\mathcal{F}\left(\left\{\tfrac{1}{m},\tfrac{m-1}{m}\right\}\right)
. We construct continuous piecewise linear functions
\hat{f}(\cdot,i):[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\hat{f}(0,i)=\hat{f}(1,i)=0
for all
i
, and
\hat{f}
satisfies
\mathcal{F}([0,1])
.

Construction of
\hat{f}(\cdot,i)
:

Let
B=\{x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\}=\{0,\tfrac{1}{m},\tfrac{m-1}{m},1\}
. Define
\hat{f}(x,i)
by linear interpolation over
B
:
For
x\in[0,1]
,

\hat{f}(x,i)=\sum_{t}\lambda_{t}f(x_{t},i)\text{
where }\sum_{t}\lambda_{t}=1,\lambda_{t}\geq 0,\mathrm{SOS2}(\lambda),

where
\mathrm{SOS2}(\lambda)
is a Special Ordered Set of type 2, which means that at most two
\lambda_{t}
are positive and if so, then they must be consecutive.
Since
\hat{f}(0,i)=\hat{f}(1,i)=0
, and
f(\tfrac{1}{m},i)
,
f(\tfrac{m-1}{m},i)
are given,
\hat{f}(x,i)
is continuous and piecewise linear in
x
.

Verification that the system is satisfied:

For all
x\in[0,1]
and
k\in K
, we need to show
\Delta_{k}\hat{f}(x)=0
(see (
14
)).
On each interval
[x_{t},x_{t+1}]
, the functions
\hat{f}(x,i)
and
\hat{f}(1-x,j)
are linear, so the left-hand side
\Delta_{k}\hat{f}(x)
is linear in
x
. At the breakpoints
x_{t}
, we have
\Delta_{k}\hat{f}(x_{t})=0

because the original solution satisfies the system at
x=\tfrac{1}{m},\tfrac{m-1}{m}
, and
\hat{f}(0,i)=\hat{f}(1,i)=0
.
Therefore, the left-hand side is zero at both ends of each interval, implying it is zero throughout the interval. Thus,
\hat{f}
satisfies
\mathcal{F}([0,1])
, and (2) holds.

Step 3:
We show that (1) implies (3).

Suppose there is a nontrivial
f\in\mathcal{F}([0,1])
. Then there exists an index
i\in[\ell]
and a point
\bar{x}\in[0,1]
such that
f(\bar{x},i)\neq 0
.
Without loss of generality,
\bar{x}\in[0,\tfrac{1}{2}]
and
i=1
.

Let
\bar{D}=\{\bar{x},1-\bar{x}\}
. By
5.0
, the function values used in the equation system
\mathcal{F}(\bar{D})
are restricted to
f(x,i)
for
(x,i)\in\mathcal{O}(\bar{x})
.
Since
f\in\mathcal{F}([0,1])
, it follows that
f|_{\bar{D}\times[\ell]}\in\mathcal{F}(\bar{D})
.

Next, let
\tilde{f}\colon\tilde{D}\times[\ell]\to\mathbb{R}
with
\tilde{D}=\{\tfrac{1}{m},\tfrac{m-1}{m}\}
be defined as

\tilde{f}(\tfrac{1}{m},i)=f(\bar{x},i),\qquad\tilde{f}(\tfrac{m-1}{m},i)=f(1-\bar{x},i).

Then
f|_{\bar{D}\times[\ell]}\in\mathcal{F}(\bar{D})
implies that
\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{F}(\tilde{D})
.

This follows by noticing that
\Delta_{k}f(\bar{x})=0
implies that
\Delta_{k}\tilde{f}(\tfrac{1}{m})=0
(and respectively for
1-\bar{x}
and
\tfrac{m-1}{m}
), which is obtained by substituting
f(\bar{x},i)
with
\tilde{f}(\tfrac{1}{m},i)
and
f(1-\bar{x},i)
with
\tilde{f}(\tfrac{m-1}{m},i)
in the system of equations.

Note that the above implication could be an “if and only if” when
\bar{x}\neq\tfrac{1}{2}
, but since we allow that option, we only make this a one-way implication.
∎

5.1.
Applying the general systems to edges

We show how two particular edge relations can be formulated in terms of (
13
). This lemma is the key to the final step of the proof of our main result.

Lemma 5.0
.

Consider Items
2
and
3
from
3.0
.
That is, consider a valid 7-tuples

\bm{\tau}^{1}=({{\text{}}}{{\text{}}}{{\text{}}}I_{1},{{\text{}}}I_{2},{{\text{}}}{{\text{}}}{{\text{}}}I_{3},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})
,

\bm{\tau}^{2}=({{\text{}}}I_{1},{{\text{}}}I_{2},{{\text{}}}I_{3},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})
.
The equations that describe a function
\bar{\pi}
being additive over
\bm{\tau}^{1}
and
\bm{\tau}^{2}
can be transformed to the format of
\mathcal{F}(D)
in (
13
).

Proof.

For each tuple, we transform the equation
\sum_{i=1}^{3}\sigma_{i}\pi({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{i})
for
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{1},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{2},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{3})\in F(\bm{\tau})
into the format of
\mathcal{F}([0,1])
.
By
3.0
,
F(\bm{\tau}^{1})
and
F(\bm{\tau}^{2})
are both 1-dimensional polytopes that can be parametrized by some variable
\lambda\in[0,1]
.
For each
\bm{\tau}^{1},\bm{\tau}^{2}
, by setting
f(\lambda,i):=\pi(\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{i}+\frac{1}{q}\lambda\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{i})
for
\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}^{i}
and
\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf d$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf d$}}}^{i}
given from the formulas, in
3.0
, we see that we exactly recover the format of the systems of equations
\mathcal{F}([0,1])
.
This completes the proof.
∎

6.
Space of Perturbations and 7-tuples

6.1.
Systems of equations

We now consider the set of valid 7-tuples
\mathcal{E}(\pi,\mathcal{P}_{q})
and subdivide it by types of 7-tuples. This combinatorial representation can then be made finite by choosing representatives of
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
in
[0,1]^{2}
under the action of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\times\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

For

{\text{}},{\text{}},{\text{}}\in\{{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}\}
, define

\mathcal{E}_{{\text{}},{\text{}},{\text{}}}:=\bigcup\{(I_{\phi(1)},I_{\phi(2)},I_{\phi(3)},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})\in\mathcal{E}\mid I_{\phi(i)}\in{\text{\hbox{\hbox to9.01pt{\vbox to9.01pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-1.49165pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{

where
\mathrm{Perm}(\{1,2,3\})
is the set of permutation functions on
\{1,2,3\}
.

For example,

\mathcal{E}_{{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}}=\{(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})\in\mathcal{E}:I_{i}\in{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}\}.

So

(15)

\mathcal{E}=\bigcup_{{\text{}},{\text{}},{\text{}}\in\{{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}\}}\mathcal{E}_{{\text{}},{\text{}},{\text{}}}.

Remark 6.0
.

By the definition of additivity over a valid 7-tuple, if
\bar{\pi}
is additive over a valid 7-tuple
(I,J,K,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},t)
, then for any valid 7-tuple
(I^{\prime},J^{\prime},K^{\prime},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})
with
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
,
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
,
K^{\prime}\subseteq K
with
I^{\prime},J^{\prime},K^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{q}
, we have
\bar{\pi}
is additive over
(I^{\prime},J^{\prime},K^{\prime},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})
.

6.2.
Restricting to the lattice when working on restricted domain

We need to adapt
[
8
,
9
, Lemma 3.14]
to a more general setting that handles working on a restricted domain and uses more general constraints.

In our proof structure of our main result, we reduce the domain by deducing certain relations about corresponding functions. So it could be that we work with only a partial triangulation of the space.

We will need the following notation for the next result. Define
\bm{\sigma}\cdot\pi\colon\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
by
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{1},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{2},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{3})\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{3}\sigma_{i}\pi({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{i})
. Note that for
\bm{\sigma}=(1,1,-1)
, we have
\bm{\sigma}\cdot\pi({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{1},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{2},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{1}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{2})=\pi({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{1})+\pi({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{2})-\pi({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{1}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{2})
.

For a polytope
P
, let
\verts(P)
denote the vertices of
P
. For a polyhedral complex
\mathcal{P}
, let
\verts(\mathcal{P}):=\bigcup\{\verts(P):P\in\mathcal{P}\}
.

A triangulation
\mathcal{T}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a polyhedral complex such that every
P\in\mathcal{T}
is a simplex of some dimension
1\leq d\leq n
or the empty set.

For a function
\phi\colon\verts(\mathcal{T})\to\mathbb{R}
, we define the piecewise linear interpolation
\mathrm{pwl}(\phi)
by

{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}\mapsto\sum_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}\in\verts(P)}\lambda_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}\phi({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}})

where
P
is a minimal face of
\mathcal{T}
containing
\textstyle\bf x
,
\sum_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}\in\verts(P)}\lambda_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}
and
\sum_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}\in\verts(P)}\lambda_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}=1
,
\lambda_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}\geq 0
.

The choice of
\lambda_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}
is unique since
\mathcal{T}
is a triangulation and
P
is chosen as a minimal face.

Lemma 6.0
.

Suppose
\mathcal{T}
be a subcomplex of a triangulation of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that there exists
q\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\verts(\mathcal{T})\subseteq\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{k}
.
Let
\mathcal{E}
be a set of valid 7-tuples
\bm{\tau}=(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3},\bm{\sigma},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})
with
I_{i}\in\mathcal{T}
,
\bm{\sigma}\in\{-1,1\}^{3}
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}\in\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{k}
. Suppose that for each
\bm{\tau}\in\mathcal{E}
we have that
p_{i}(F(\bm{\tau}))\subseteq\verts(\mathcal{T})
for
i=1,2,3
.

(1)

If
\bar{\pi}\in\bar{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{T}}
, then
\bar{\pi}|_{\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\in\bar{\Pi}^{{\mathcal{E}}\cap\frac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{k}}_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\frac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{k},\mathcal{T}\cap\frac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{k}}
.

(2)

Let
\bar{\pi}_{\mathcal{T}}
be piecewise linear over
\mathcal{T}
.
Then

\bar{\pi}_{\mathcal{T}}\in\bar{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{T}}
if and only if

\bar{\pi}_{\mathcal{T}}|_{\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\in\bar{\Pi}^{{\mathcal{E}}\cap\frac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{k}}_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\frac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{k},\mathcal{T}\cap\frac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{k}}
.

(3)

For every
\bar{\pi}\in\bar{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z}}
there exists unique
\bar{\pi}_{\mathcal{T}}\in\bar{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{T}}
and
\bar{\pi}_{0}\in\bar{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z}\cup\verts(\mathcal{T}),\mathcal{T}}

such that

\bar{\pi}=\bar{\pi}_{\mathcal{T}}+\bar{\pi}_{0}.

Proof.

(1)

Trivially,
\bar{\pi}
must be additive over
\mathcal{E}\cap\frac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, i.e.,
\bm{\sigma}\cdot\bar{\pi}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{1},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{2},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{3})=0
for all
\sum_{i=1}^{3}\sigma_{i}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{i}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{i}\in\frac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, where
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{i}\in I_{i}
and
\bm{\tau}=(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})\in\mathcal{E}
. But since
\verts(\mathcal{T})\subseteq\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, we must have that
\bar{\pi}|_{\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{k}}
is additive over
\mathcal{E}\cap\frac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{k}
.

(2)

Let
\bm{\tau}=(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3},\bm{\sigma},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})\in\mathcal{E}
be a valid 7-tuple.
Since
\bm{\tau}
is a valid 7-tuple,
I_{i}\in\mathcal{T}
. And since
\bar{\pi}_{\mathcal{T}}
is piecewise linear over
\mathcal{T}
, we have that
\bm{\sigma}\cdot\pi_{\mathcal{T}}
is affine over
F(\bm{\tau})
.
Thus,

\bm{\sigma}\cdot\pi_{\mathcal{T}}|_{F(\bm{\tau})}\equiv 0

if and only if

\bm{\sigma}\cdot\pi_{\mathcal{T}}|_{\verts(F(\bm{\tau}))}\equiv 0.

Since
p_{i}(\verts(F(\bm{\tau})))\subseteq\verts(\mathcal{T})\subseteq\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, we have that

\bm{\sigma}\cdot\pi_{\mathcal{T}}|_{\verts(F(\bm{\tau}))}\equiv 0

if and only if

\left(\bm{\sigma}\cdot\bar{\pi}_{\mathcal{T}}|_{\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\right)\bigg\rvert_{\verts(F(\bm{\tau}))}\equiv 0.

Noting this for each
\bm{\tau}\in\mathcal{E}
completes the proof of part (2).

(3)

Next, let
\bar{\pi}\in\bar{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z}}
. Let
\bar{\pi}_{\mathcal{T}}
be the unique extension of
\bar{\pi}|_{\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{k}}
to a piecewise linear function over
\mathcal{T}
. It follows that
\bar{\pi}_{\mathcal{T}}|_{\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{k}}=\bar{\pi}|_{\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\in\bar{\Pi}^{{\mathcal{E}}\cap\frac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{k}}_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\frac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{k},\mathcal{T}\cap\frac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{k}}
.
Therefore, by part (2),
\bar{\pi}_{\mathcal{T}}\in\bar{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{T}}
. Since
\bar{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{T}}
is a vector space containing
\bar{\pi}
and
\bar{\pi}_{\mathcal{T}}
, we have that
\pi_{0}=\bar{\pi}-\bar{\pi}_{\mathcal{T}}\in\bar{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{T}}
, which establishes part (3).

∎

Remark 6.0
.

The polyhedral complexes
\mathcal{P}_{B}
for
B=\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}\cap[0,1)
from
2.3
and
\mathcal{P}_{q}
from
2.3
are triangulations of
\mathbb{R}^{1}
and
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, respectively, and satisfy the hypotheses of
6.0
. This is due to
3.0
.

6.3.
Mappings on restriction and interpolation

We construct a diagram to illustrate the interaction between restriction and interpolation across various perturbation spaces. This connection plays a crucial role in our main proof by demonstrating their correspondence, enabling us to relate these spaces while modifying the function’s domain to simplify our arguments.

Lemma 6.0
.

Suppose
\mathcal{P}
is a
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-periodic collection of faces such that
p(\mathcal{E})\subseteq\mathcal{P}\hskip 1.0pt{\textnormal{{\#}}}\hskip 1.0pt(\mathcal{P}_{q,{{\text{}}}}\cup\mathcal{P}_{q,{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}})
, and
\verts(\mathcal{P}_{q})\subseteq\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
.
Then for
m\geq 1
, the following maps in the diagram exist:

(1)

a surjection by restriction
\phi_{1}\colon\pi\mapsto\pi|_{\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
from
{\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{P}}}
to
{\bar{\Pi}^{{\mathcal{E}}\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2},\mathcal{P}\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}}
;

(2)

a bijection
by interpolation
\phi_{2}
from
{\bar{\Pi}^{{\mathcal{E}}\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2},\mathcal{P}\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}}
to
{\bar{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{P}}\cap\mathrm{Pwl}(\mathcal{P}\hskip 1.0pt{\textnormal{{\#}}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{P}_{mq})}
, where
\mathrm{Pwl}(\mathcal{P}\hskip 1.0pt{\textnormal{{\#}}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{P}_{mq})
denotes the set of continuous piecewise linear functions on the refinement of
\mathcal{P}_{q}
on the domain
\mathcal{P}
;

(3)

an injection by identity embedding
\phi_{3}\colon\pi\mapsto\pi
from
{\bar{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{P}}\cap\mathrm{Pwl}(\mathcal{P}\hskip 1.0pt{\textnormal{{\#}}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{P}_{mq})}
to
{\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{P}}}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bar{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{P}}\cap\mathrm{Pwl}(\mathcal{P}\hskip 1.0pt{\textnormal{{\#}}}\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{P}_{mq})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bar{\Pi}^{{\mathcal{E}}\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2},\mathcal{P}\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{P}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{1}\textup{(restrict)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{2}\textup{ (interpolate)}}

Note that the maps (1) and (3) also exist for any valid 7-tuples
\mathcal{E}
. The main proof ingredient for map (2) is if the additivity equation holds for the discrete function, then the additivity equation holds for the interpolation function.

Proof.

Map (1).

Let
\bar{\pi}\in{\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{P}}}
, the restriction
\bar{\pi}|_{\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
is unique. The restriction map
\phi_{1}(\bar{\pi})
also inherits the additivity equations from
\bar{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{P}}
, thus
\phi_{1}(\bar{\pi})\in{\bar{\Pi}^{{\mathcal{E}}\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2},\mathcal{P}\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}}
.

To prove that
\phi_{1}
is a surjection, for any
\bar{\pi}_{mq}\in{\bar{\Pi}^{{\mathcal{E}}\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2},\mathcal{P}\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}}
, we have
\phi_{1}(\phi_{2}(\bar{\pi}_{mq}))=\bar{\pi}_{mq}
, where the interpolation
\phi_{2}(\bar{\pi}_{mq})
is in
{\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{P}}}
.

Map (2).
Let
\bar{\pi}_{mq}\colon P\cap\tfrac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
. Let
\mathrm{pwl}(\bar{\pi}_{mq})
be the continuous piecewise linear function obtained from
\bar{\pi}_{mq}
via interpolation. By
6.0

item 2
,
\bar{\pi}_{mq}\in{\bar{\Pi}^{{\mathcal{E}}\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2},\mathcal{P}\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}}
if and only if

\mathrm{pwl}(\bar{\pi}_{mq})\in\bar{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{P}}
. Since
\mathrm{pwl}(\bar{\pi}_{mq})|_{\tfrac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}=\bar{\pi}_{mq}
, we conclude that
\phi_{2}
is indeed a bijection.

Map (3).
Clearly any piecewise linear function is continuous, so the mapping as an injection is trivial.
∎

7.
Proof of main result

We state and prove our main result about the connection of piecewise linear 2D functions with the finite group problem.

We will often derive results about perturbation functions when they are continuous. For this we define the set of continuous perturbation functions

\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{P}}:=\{\bar{\pi}\in\bar{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{P}}:\bar{\pi}\text{ is Lipschitz continuous}\}.

As functions in this space are continuous, we can think of
\mathcal{Z}
as a polyhedral complex instead of just a list of polytopes. Thus, for any
I\in\mathcal{Z}
, we can assume that any subface
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
is also contained in
\mathcal{Z}
.

Theorem 7.1

(Main Result)
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 3}
. Let
\pi
be a continuous piecewise linear minimal
valid function for
R_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf f$}}}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{Z}^{2})
over
\mathcal{P}_{q}
and suppose
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf f$}}}\in\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

\pi
is a facet for
R_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf f$}}}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{Z}^{2})
,

(2)

\pi
is extreme for
R_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf f$}}}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{Z}^{2})
,

(3)

\pi|_{\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
is extreme for
R_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf f$}}}(\tfrac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2},\mathbb{Z}^{2})
.

The proof will track the space of perturbation functions as we transform additivities and restrict the space. Thus, the technique here will require moving between different function spaces and maintaining a connection between these spaces.

We focus on proving that
3
implies
1
.

Proof of
Theorem 7.1
.

As mentioned in
subsubsection 2.2.4
, facets are extreme functions
[
11
, Lemma 1.3]
, and hence
1

\Rightarrow

2
.
By
Theorem 2.9
,
2

\Rightarrow

3
.
We now show
3

\Rightarrow

1
.

Let
\pi|_{\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
be extreme for
R_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf f$}}}(\tfrac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2},\mathbb{Z}^{2})
.
Since the domain is finite, we know by
Theorem 2.7
that

(16)

\bar{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}(\pi)\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}_{\emptyset,\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}=\{0\}.

For the sake of deriving a contradiction,
suppose that
\pi
is not a facet for
R_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf f$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf f$}}}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{Z}^{2})
. By the Facet Theorem (
Theorem 2.3
), this means that

(17)

\bar{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}(\pi)}\neq\{0\}.

We will show that (
16
) and (
17
) are in contradiction.

Since
\pi
is piecewise linear,
\pi
is Lipschitz continuous. By
Theorem 2.2
, any
\bar{\pi}
in
\bar{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}(\pi)}
is also Lipschitz continuous. Furthermore, since
\pi
is piecewise linear, we can combinatorialize the additivities via
2.3

we can write

\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}(\pi,\mathcal{P}_{q})}_{\emptyset,\mathcal{P}_{q}}=\bar{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}(\pi)}.

If there exists
\bar{\pi}\in\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}(\pi,\mathcal{P}_{q})}_{\emptyset,\mathcal{P}_{q}}
with
\bar{\pi}|_{\frac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\neq 0
, then by
6.0
part
2
with
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{P}_{mq}
, there is a nontrivial
\bar{\pi}|_{\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\in\bar{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}(\pi)\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}_{\emptyset,\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
, which contradicts (
16
).
Therefore
\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}(\pi,\mathcal{P}_{q})}_{\frac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{2},\mathcal{P}_{q}}=\bar{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}(\pi)}\neq\{0\}
.

By
3.0
, we know the exact structure of
\mathcal{E}(\pi,\mathcal{P}_{q})
and can expand this to a union of 7-tuples as (
15
). We will sequentially address these types of 7-tuples - we will modify additive 7-tuples, the zero that we enforce, and the domain to obtain simpler perturbations spaces that we can analyze.

We will initialize

\mathcal{E}\leftarrow\mathcal{E}(\pi,\mathcal{P}_{q}),\ \ \mathcal{Z}\leftarrow\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{2},\ \ \mathcal{P}\leftarrow\mathcal{P}_{q}

and maintain a triple
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{P})
such that

(18)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{P}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{project}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{lift}}

(1)

As we update
\mathcal{E}\leftarrow\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\mathcal{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
, we will say that the
update is equal
provided that
\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{P}}=\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}}
.

(2)

As we update
\mathcal{E}\leftarrow\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\mathcal{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{Z}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}\leftarrow\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, we will say that the
update is equivalent
provided that (
18
) holds.

Clearly an equal update is equivalent.

For triple
(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},\mathcal{Z}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
that projects, we can derive a contradiction by showing that

\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2},\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\neq\{0\}\ \ \Rightarrow\ \ \widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}(\pi,\mathcal{P}_{q})\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}_{\frac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{2},\mathcal{P}_{q}\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\neq\{0\}\ \ \Rightarrow\ \ \widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}(\pi)\cap\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}_{\emptyset,\frac{1}{mq}\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\neq\{0\}.

Throughout we assume
\bar{\pi}\in\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}(\pi)}\setminus\{0\}
as our representative that
\pi
is not a facet.

Before the mathematical description of each step, we will provide a pictorial description and a summary to explain the concepts of that step.
In the diagrams, we use green to denote faces of
\mathcal{P}_{q}
in 7-tuple additivity, red to denote when they must take the value zero in the perturbation space, and a crosshatch on triangles when we remove it from the domain of the function via a projection. The diagrams do not represent
\textstyle\bf\sigma
and
\textstyle\bf t
since these are not key elements in the arguments (although they are important in definitions of valid 7-tuples).

Edge Additive
Zero
Project

Step 1: Remove
\mathcal{E}_{{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}}}
.

Diagram

Update

(no diagram needed for this step)

We deduced already that
\bar{\pi}|_{\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}}=0
. Thus,
\bar{\pi}
is trivially additive over any tuple with points from the grid.

\mathcal{E}\leftarrow\mathcal{E}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}}}

(Equivalent update)

Now we assume that
\mathcal{E}_{{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}}}=\emptyset
.

Lemma 7.1

(Step 1 equal)
.

The update
\mathcal{E}\leftarrow\mathcal{E}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{E}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}}}
,
\mathcal{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{Z}
is an equal update.

Proof.

Because
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
,
\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{P}}\subseteq\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{P}}
.

Alternatively, for any
\tilde{\pi}\in\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{P}}
,
\tilde{\pi}
trivially is additive over any
\bm{\tau}\in\mathcal{E}_{{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}}}
, and thus
\tilde{\pi}\in\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{P}}
.
∎

Step 2: Remove full dimensional additivities.

\Leftrightarrow
\Leftrightarrow

Step 2a. Remove
\mathcal{E}_{{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}}
:

Use the generalized interval lemma to show that perturbations vanish on these triangles.

Step 2b. Remove
\mathcal{E}_{{\text{\hbox{\hbox to6.43pt{\vbox to6.43pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{

Set

\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}=\bigcup_{{\text{}}\in\{{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}\}}\mathcal{E}_{{\text{}},{\text{}},{\text{}}}

Lemma 7.1

(Step 2a equal)
.

The update
\mathcal{E}\leftarrow\mathcal{E}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{E}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}}
,
\mathcal{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{Z}\cup p(\mathcal{E}_{{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}})
is equal.

Proof.

Suppose that
\bar{\pi}=0
on
I,J,K
, we know that
\bar{\pi}
satisfies trivially the equation by
(I,J,K,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf 0$}}})
. Therefore,
\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}}\subseteq\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{P}}
.
On the other hand, suppose that
\bar{\pi}\in\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{P}}
. By
Theorem 2.5
,
\bar{\pi}
are affine in the interiors of
I,J,K
. Because
\bar{\pi}
is continuous,
\bar{\pi}
is affine in
I,J,K
. As
\verts(I)\cup\verts(J)\cup\verts(K)\subseteq\mathcal{P}\cap\frac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{2}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
,
\bar{\pi}=0
in
I,J,K
. Therefore,
\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{P}}\subseteq\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}}
.
Hence,
\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{P}}=\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}}
.
∎

Lemma 7.1

(Step 2b equal)
.

The update
\mathcal{E}\leftarrow\mathcal{E}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{E}\setminus\bigcup_{{\text{}}\in\{{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}}\}}\mathcal{E}_{{\text{}},{\text{}},{\text{}}}
,
\mathcal{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{Z}\cup p(\bigcup_{{\text{}}\in\{{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}}\}}\mathcal{E}_{{\text{}},{\text{}},{\text{}}})
is equal.

Proof.

Clearly if
\bar{\pi}=0
in
I_{i}\text{ for }i=1,2,3
, then
\bar{\pi}
is additive over
(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})
. On the other hand, if
\bar{\pi}
is additive over
(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})
, then by
2.6
,
\bar{\pi}
is affine on
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}
. Since
\bar{\pi}
is zero on the vertices of
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}
, we have that
\bar{\pi}
is zero on all
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}
.
Therefore,
\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{P}}=\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}}
.
∎

Step 3: Remove
\mathcal{E}_{{{\text{}}}{{\text{}}}{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}}
.

\Leftrightarrow

Consider some
\bm{\tau}^{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{{{\text{}}}{{\text{}}}{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}}
. We use
4.0
to deduce affine properties in the direction of the edge. This allows us to set some edges to 0.
We then recover an equivalent update by adding appropriate 7-tuples.

We iteratively apply this logic.

For
\bm{\tau}^{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{{\text{}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}}

with
*\in\{{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}}\}

find appropriate
\bm{\tau}^{1},\bm{\tau}^{2},\bm{\tau}^{3}
that each use at most 1 triangle.

\mathcal{E}\leftarrow(\mathcal{E}\setminus\{\bm{\tau}^{0}\})\cup\{\bm{\tau}^{1},\bm{\tau}^{2},\bm{\tau}^{3}\}

\mathcal{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{Z}\cup\{{\text{}}I_{1},{\text{}}J,{\text{}}K_{1}\}

Now we assume
\mathcal{E}_{{{\text{}}}{{\text{}}}{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}}=\emptyset
.

Note, at this point, for any
\bm{\tau}\in\mathcal{E}
, we have
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf 0$}}}
.

Lemma 7.1

(Step 3 equal)
.

For each
\bm{\tau}^{0}=({{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}I,{\text{}}J,{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}K,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf 0$}}})\in\mathcal{E}
with
I,K\in\mathcal{P}_{q,{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}}
,
J\in\mathcal{P}_{q,{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}}
,
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}\in\{(1,1,-1),(1,-1,1)\}
,
there exist 7-tuples
\{\bm{\tau}^{1},\bm{\tau}^{2},\bm{\tau}^{3}\}
where

\bm{\tau}^{1},\bm{\tau}^{2}
are of type (6) and
\bm{\tau}^{3}
is of type (2) such that
the update
\mathcal{E}\leftarrow\mathcal{E}^{\prime}:=(\mathcal{E}\setminus\{\bm{\tau}^{0}\})\cup\{\bm{\tau}^{1},\bm{\tau}^{2},\bm{\tau}^{3}\}
,
\mathcal{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{Z}\cup\{{\text{}}I_{1},{\text{}}J,{\text{}}K_{1}\}
is equal.

Proof.

We will prove the lemma only for
J\in\mathcal{P}_{q,{{\text{}}}}
as the other cases are similar.

[Define subfaces of
I
and
K
].

Let
I_{1},K_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{q,{{\text{}}}}
,
I_{2},K_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{q,{{\text{}}}}
,
I_{3},K_{3}\in\mathcal{P}_{q,{{\text{}}}}
such that
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}\subseteq I
, and
K_{1},K_{2},K_{3}\subseteq K
.

[Deduce 0’s from interval lemma].

By
4.0
, if
\bar{\pi}
is additive on
({{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}I,{{\text{}}}J,{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}K,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf 0$}}})
, then
\bar{\pi}
is affine over
I,J,K
in the
-direction. By continuity and
\bar{\pi}|_{\verts(\mathcal{P}_{q})}\equiv 0
, we know that
\bar{\pi}(x,y)=\bar{\pi}(a,y)
for all
(x,y)\in I
,
\bar{\pi}(x,y)=\bar{\pi}(b,y)
for all
(x,y)\in K
, and
\bar{\pi}|_{I_{1}\cup J\cup K_{1}}\equiv 0
.
Equally, if
\bar{\pi}|_{I_{1}\cup J\cup K_{1}}\equiv 0
, then
\bar{\pi}
is additive on
({{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}I,{{\text{}}}J,{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}K,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf 0$}}})
.
Thus, since
\bm{\tau}^{0}\in\mathcal{E}
, the update
\mathcal{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{Z}\cup\{I_{1},J,K_{1}\}
is equal.

[Show equivalent tuples].

Next, assuming the updated
\mathcal{Z}
, pick a vertex
\textstyle\bf u
of
J
and the unique vectors
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}^{i}
according to
3.0
, to make these valid 7-tuples:

\begin{array}[]{rlllll}\bm{\tau}^{1}&:=({{\text{}}}J,&{{\text{}}}I_{2},&{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}I,&\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},&{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}^{1}),\\
\bm{\tau}^{2}&:=({{\text{}}}J,&{{\text{}}}K_{2},&{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}K,&\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},&{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}^{2}),\\
\bm{\tau}^{3}&:=({{\text{}}}I_{2},&{{\text{}}}\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}\},&{{\text{}}}K_{2},&\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},&{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf 0$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf 0$}}}).\end{array}

We want to show that
{\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}}}={\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}}}
.

First, suppose
\bar{\pi}\in\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}}
.
Because
\bar{\pi}=0
on
I_{1},J,K_{1}
,
\bar{\pi}
is invariant over changes in the
-direction. Hence,
\bm{\tau}^{1}
and
\bm{\tau}^{2}
are satisfied. By
6.0
, since
\bar{\pi}
is additive on
\bm{\tau}
, then it is also additive on
\bm{\tau}^{3}
.
Thus,
\bar{\pi}\in\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}}
.

Next, suppose
\bar{\pi}\in\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}}
. We just need to show that
\bar{\pi}
is additive on
\bm{\tau}
. Suppose that
({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{1},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{2},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{3})\in F(\bm{\tau})
. Because
\bar{\pi}
is additive on
\bm{\tau}^{3}
, we have
\sigma_{1}\bar{\pi}(\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}}^{1})+\sigma_{3}\bar{\pi}(\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}}^{3})=0
, where
\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}}^{1}
is the projection of
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{1}
onto
I_{2}
and
\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}}^{3}
is the projection of
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{3}
onto
K_{2}
. Because
\bar{\pi}
is additive on
\bm{\tau}^{1}
and
\bar{\pi}=0
on
J
, we have
\sigma_{2}\bar{\pi}(\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}}^{1})+\sigma_{3}\bar{\pi}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{1})=0
. Similarly, because
\bar{\pi}
is additive on
\bm{\tau}^{2}
and
\bar{\pi}=0
on
J
, we have
\sigma_{2}\bar{\pi}(\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}}^{3})+\sigma_{3}\bar{\pi}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{3})=0
.
Thus,
\sigma_{1}\bar{\pi}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{1})+\sigma_{2}\bar{\pi}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{2})+\sigma_{3}\bar{\pi}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}^{3})=-\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}(\sigma_{1}\bar{\pi}(\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}}^{1})+\sigma_{3}\bar{\pi}(\tilde{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf x$}}}}^{3}))=0
, which implies that
\bar{\pi}\in\widehat{\Pi}^{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}}
.

Therefore, the update
\mathcal{E}\leftarrow\mathcal{E}\setminus\{\bm{\tau}^{0}\}\cup\{\bm{\tau}^{1},\bm{\tau}^{2},\bm{\tau}^{3}\}
is an equal update.

∎

Corollary 7.1

(Updates applied iteratively)
.

By iteratively applying
7.1
, we create an equal update with
\mathcal{E}_{{{\text{}}}{{\text{}}}{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}}=\emptyset
.

Step 4: Remove
\mathcal{E}_{{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}}
by changing the domain.

\Rightarrow
\Leftrightarrow
\Leftrightarrow
\Leftrightarrow

Consider some
\bm{\tau}\in\mathcal{E}_{{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}}
where

\bm{\tau}=({{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}I,{{\text{}}}\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}\},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}K,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})
.

We will remove
I
from the domain. To do so, we need to do the following:

(1) If
I\in\mathcal{Z}
, add
K
to
\mathcal{Z}
.

(2) Remove
\bm{\tau}
from
\mathcal{E}
.

(3) For any
\bm{\tau}^{\prime}
with
I
in it, replace
I
by
K
to create a new 7-tuple
\bm{\tau}^{\prime\prime}
.
(Note, we need to update
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}^{\prime},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}^{\prime}
via
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}
and
\textstyle\bf u
.).

(4) By storing
\bm{\tau}
, we can retain a mapping for how to recover function values on
I
, and for now remove
I
from the domain.

[This step repeats until there are no more
\bm{\tau}\in\mathcal{E}_{{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}}
. The sequence as to choices of
\bm{\tau}
to apply this to is not important, even though it will result in different representations.]

While
\mathcal{E}_{{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}}\neq\emptyset
, let
\bm{\tau}\in\mathcal{E}_{{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}}
.

(1) If
I\in\mathcal{Z}
, then

\mathcal{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{Z}\setminus\{I\}\cup\{K\}
.

(2)
\mathcal{E}\leftarrow\mathcal{E}\setminus\{\bm{\tau}\}
.

(3) For all
\bm{\tau}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}
containing
I
, derive
\bm{\tau}^{\prime\prime}
and

\mathcal{E}\leftarrow\mathcal{E}\setminus\{\bm{\tau}^{\prime}\}\cup\{\bm{\tau}^{\prime\prime}\}
.

(4)
\mathcal{P}\leftarrow\mathcal{P}\setminus\{{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}I\}
.

Now we assume
\mathcal{E}_{{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}},{{\text{}}},{{\text{}}\,{\text{}}}}=\emptyset
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Suppose that
\bm{\tau}=(I,{{\text{}}}\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}\},K,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})\in\mathcal{E}
where
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}\in\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Then
\mathcal{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{Z}\setminus\{I\}\cup\{K\}
is an equal update.

Proof.

This equivalence is obvious by exchanging function values through the additivity over
\bm{\tau}
.
∎

Lemma 7.1
.

Suppose that
\bm{\tau}=(I_{1},{{\text{}}}\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}\},\tilde{I}_{1},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})\in\mathcal{E}
where
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}\in\tfrac{1}{q}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
Suppose that
\bm{\tau}^{\prime}=(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\mu$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\mu$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\mu$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\mu$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}})\in\mathcal{E}
containing
I_{1}
. Then there exists a valid 7-tuple
\bm{\tau}^{\prime\prime}=(\tilde{I}_{1},I_{2},I_{3},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\tilde{\mu}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\tilde{\mu}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\tilde{\mu}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\tilde{\mu}$}},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\tilde{u}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\tilde{u}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\tilde{u}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\tilde{u}$}})
with
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\tilde{\mu}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\tilde{\mu}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\tilde{\mu}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\tilde{\mu}$}}=(\tilde{\mu}_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3})
, where
\tilde{\mu}_{1}=-\sigma_{1}\sigma_{3}\mu_{1}
and
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\tilde{u}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\tilde{u}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\tilde{u}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\tilde{u}$}}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf u$}}}-\mu_{1}\sigma_{1}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}}+\mu_{1}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}
, such that
\mathcal{E}\leftarrow\mathcal{E}\setminus\{\bm{\tau}^{\prime}\}\cup\{\bm{\tau}^{\prime\prime}\}
is an equal update.

Proof.

Additivity.
Since
\pi
is additive over
(I_{1},{{\text{}}}\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf v$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf v$}}}\},\tilde{I}_{1},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf\sigma$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf\sigma$}},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\bf t$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\bf t$}}})
, we know that
